(b)

Signed covering of
G

Figure 27
:
A graph for which the generating sets from
Theorem
14.5
and Theorem
14.11
differ

The following proposition summarizes the results of this section, linking the
generating sets of Theorems
14.5
,
14.9
,
and
14.11
.

Proposition 14.12
.

Suppose
G
is a finite connected graph. There are then three generating
sets,
S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset S_{3}

for the toric ideal
I_{G}
:

•

S_{1}=\{T_{w}\colon w\text{ an even closed walk in the forms
of Theorem~\ref{thm:ohsugihibi}}\}

•

S_{2}=\{U(C)\colon C\text{ a cycle in the signed covering}\}

•

S_{3}=\{T_{w}\colon w\text{ an even closed walk}\}
.

15
Some Complete Intersection Rings
R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}

Now that one understands the toric ideal, one can turn one’s attention to the
question of computing
\Psi_{P}
and
\Psi_{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}
via complete intersection
presentations.
This section gives factorizations of

\displaystyle\Psi_{P}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}w\left(\dfrac{1}{(x_{1}-x_{2})(x_{2}-x_{3})\cdots(x_{n-1}-x_{n})x_{n}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}w\left(\dfrac{1}{(x_{1}-x_{2})(x_{2}-x_{3})\cdots(x_{n-1}-x_{n})2x_{n}}\right)

for a certain class of signed posets using Proposition
6.14
.

Recall Theorem
1.1
, where Greene showed that, for a strongly planar poset,

\Psi_{P}(\bm{x})=\dfrac{\prod_{\rho}(x_{\min(\rho)}-x_{\max(\rho)})}{\prod_{i\lessdot j}(x_{i}-x_{j})},

where
\rho
runs over all the regions enclosed by the Hasse diagram of
P
and
i\lessdot j
over the covering relations of the poset.
[
8
]
showed that a similar factorization occurs
for posets that are “gluings of diamonds along chains” and, in particular,
for
strongly planar posets, recovering Greene’s result.
In
[
9
]
, Boussicault, Féray, Lascoux and
Reiner gave an algebraic explanation for the disconnecting chains
of
[
8
]
by showing certain posets (including strongly planar
posets) are complete intersections by constructing a regular sequence using the
operation of opening/closing a notch.

The situation for signed posets is quite similar. We will construct a regular
sequence by opening/closing signed notches, culminating in
Theorem
15.12
.

Theorem
15.12
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset such
that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has an embedding in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
which is
strongly planar. Then

•

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has an embedding
that is both centrally symmetric and strongly planar,

•

R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
(resp.
R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
) is a complete intersection, with
I_{P}

(resp.
I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
) generated by the cycle binomials of the cycles in

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
) defining the faces of the graph, and

•

one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod_{\rho}(1-\bm{x}^{\rho})}{\prod_{e}(1-x_{a}^{\delta}x_{b}^{-\epsilon})}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod_{\rho}(1-\bm{x}^{\rho})}{\prod_{e}(1-x_{a}^{\delta}x_{b}^{-\epsilon})},

where
\rho
runs over all regions enclosed by
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
)
not fixed by the involution,
e
runs over the orbits of edges
\delta a\to\epsilon b
(
a<b
) in
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
,
x_{0}
is taken to be
1
and

\bm{x}^{\rho}=x_{\min(\rho)}^{\mathrm{sgn}(\min(\rho))}x_{\max(\rho)}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\max(\rho))}.

The next several sections will build up to the proof of Theorem
15.12
in Section
15.3
, where what it means for a signed poset to be strongly planar will be defined.

•

Section
15.1
will reduce to the case of signed
posets consisting of a single biconnected component (see
Definition
15.1
and Proposition
15.2
) and whose root cones are full-dimensional (see Propositions
15.3
and
15.4
).

•

Section
15.2
defines the notion of a signed
notch (see Definition
15.5
), explains what it means to
open/close a signed notch and what impact that
has on the root cone semigroup ring (see
Proposition
15.9
).

•

Section
15.3
defines strongly planar
posets (see Definition
15.10
), proves a
number of propositions and lemmas regarding strongly planar
posets and then proves Theorem
15.12
.

•

After the proof, Section
15.4
will explain
how Theorem
15.12
can be used to compute
\Psi_{P}

and
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
.

15.1
A few reductions

The first reduction will be to the case of signed posets consisting of a single
biconnected component.

Definition 15.1
.

Let
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
and
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
be signed posets. Let
A\subset P

(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
) be the elements corresponding to the edges of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
). Say two elements of
A
are
cycle equivalent
if there is
a cycle in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
) not fixed orientation-wise by the involution passing
through an edge corresponding to each element. Taking the transitive closure
gives an equivalence relation. Combine equivalence classes lying in the same
orbit of the involution. This partitions the edges of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(rep.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) into the
biconnected components
of
P
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
).
Each biconnected component corresponds to a signed poset, which will also be
called the biconnected components of
P
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
).

Consider
P=\{+e_{1}-e_{2},+e_{1}+e_{2},+e_{1}-e_{3},+e_{1}+e_{3},+e_{1}-e_{4},+e_{1}-e_{5},+e_{1}-e_{6},+e_{2}-e_{3},+e_{2}-e_{4},+e_{2}-e_{5},+e_{2}-e_{6},+e_{4}-e_{6},+e_{5}-e_{6},+e_{1}\}
.
Figure
28
shows
\widehat{G}(P)
with the edges of one biconnected
component solid and the edges of the other dashed.

1
2
3
-3
-2
-1
5
4
6
-5
-4
-6
0

(a)

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)

1
2
3
-3
-2
-1
5
4
6
-5
-4
-6

(b)

\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})

Figure 28
:
\widehat{G}(P)
for
P=\{+e_{1}-e_{2},+e_{1}+e_{2},+e_{1}-e_{3},+e_{1}+e_{3},+e_{1}-e_{4},+e_{1}-e_{5},+e_{1}-e_{6},+e_{2}-e_{3},+e_{2}-e_{4},+e_{2}-e_{5},+e_{2}-e_{6},+e_{4}-e_{6},+e_{5}-e_{6},+e_{1}\}
with biconnected
components indicated by dashed and solid lines.

Proposition 15.2
.

Let
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) be a signed poset and
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
(resp.
P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{1},\ldots,P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{k}
) its biconnected components. Then

R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong R_{P_{1}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes R_{P_{k}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong R_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{1}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}R_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{k}}^{\mathrm{rt}}

and, as a consequence,

\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{P_{i}}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{i}}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x}).

Proof.

Recall that
R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}=S_{P}/I_{P}
and
R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}=S_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}/I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
, where

I_{P}
and
I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
are generated, respectively, by the cycle binomials of cycles of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
not fixed by the involution. Every edge in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
lies in a unique biconnected component and is thus associated to a unique
P_{i}
(resp.
P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{i}
). Consequently,
S_{P}\cong\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{k}S_{P_{\ell}}
and
S_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\cong\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{k}S_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{\ell}}
. Furthermore, each cycle not fixed by the involution lies wholly in a single biconnected component. Consequently,
I_{P}=\bigoplus_{\ell=1}^{k}I_{P_{k}}
and
I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}=\bigoplus_{\ell=1}^{k}I_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{k}}
. Then

R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong S_{P}/I_{P}=S/\oplus_{\ell=1}^{k}I_{P_{k}}\cong\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{k}S_{P_{\ell}}/I_{P_{\ell}}\cong R_{P_{1}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}R_{P_{k}}^{\mathrm{rt}}

and

R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong S_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}/I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}=S/\oplus_{\ell=1}^{k}I_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{k}}\cong\bigotimes_{\ell=1}^{k}S_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{\ell}}/I_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{\ell}}\cong R_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{1}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}R_{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}_{k}}^{\mathrm{rt}},

as claimed.
∎

For the remainder of this chapter, it will be assumed that all signed posets
consist of a single biconnected component. In particular, one may assume that

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
have no vertices of degree one.

Furthermore, signed posets for which
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is not full-dimensional need not be
considered here, as they reduce to work of
[
9
]
, as is now explained.

Suppose
P
is a signed poset such that
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is not full-dimensional. There
are two possibilities:

•

P
can be relabeled so that
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n-1}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n-1}})
, i.e. one should think of
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
rather than
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

•

The signed graph
\Sigma_{P}
underlying the Hasse diagram of
P
is
balanced. (See Definition
8.3
for
the definition of the Hasse diagram of a signed poset and
Definition
12.12
for the notion of a balanced
signed graph.)

While the first case is straightforward, the second requires a little care.

Proposition 15.3
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset such that

\Sigma_{P}
is balanced. Then
P
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
) is isomorphic to a
poset
P^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{A_{n-1}}\cap\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
P^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{A_{n-1}}\cap\Phi_{C_{n}}
).

Proof.

One may assume
\widehat{G}(P)
consists of a single biconnected component.
Consequently,
\Sigma_{P}
must be connected. Since
\Sigma_{P}
is balanced, the
vertices can be partitioned into sets
V^{+}
and
V^{-}
such that edges labeled

+
join a vertex in
V^{+}
to one in
V^{-}
and edges labeled
-
join two
vertices in either
V^{+}
or
V^{-}
. (This is Theorem
12.17
.)
Note that since
\Sigma_{P}
is balanced, it cannot contain any self-loops.
Therefore, there is no
\pm i<\mp i
in
\widehat{G}(P)
. (In particular, should
one be considering
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
,
0
must lie in its own connected component.)
Then
\widehat{G}(P)
consists of two isotropic components (plus
0
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
).
One can then read off a signed permutation sending
P
to some
P^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{A_{n-1}}
(it is the permutation flipping signs so that all vertices in each
isotropic component have the same sign), as desired.
∎

Next, one checks that, given an
\Phi_{A_{n-1}}
-poset, its root cone semigroup does not
change when one switches among the
\Phi_{A_{n-1}}
,
\Phi_{B_{n}}
and
\Phi_{C_{n}}
root lattices.

Proposition 15.4
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{A_{n-1}}
is a poset. Then the semigroups
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}
,

K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{B}
and
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{C}
are equal.

Proof.

First, begin by observing that
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}\subset K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{B}

and
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{C}
. Suppose
\alpha\in K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{B}
. Then
\alpha

can be written as a positive integer linear combination of elements of
P

corresponding to the covering relations in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. However, these
elements correspond precisely to the covering relations in the Hasse diagram of

P
, which are a minimal generating set for
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}

(see
[
9
, Proposition 7.1]
). Therefore,
\alpha\in K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}
, so
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}=K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{B}
. The same argument
shows
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{A}=K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{C}
.
∎

As a consequence of the last two propositions, if
P
is a signed poset and

K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is not full-dimensional, its root cone semigroup ring can be understood
using the results of
[
9
]
. If
\widehat{G}(P)
is
disconnected, it either has multiple biconnected components or consists of a
single biconnected component made up of two isotropic connected components,
meaning
K_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is not full-dimensional. Consequently, for the remainder of the
chapter it will be assumed that
\widehat{G}(P)
is connected and consists of a
single biconnected component.

15.2
Signed Notches

In
[
9
]
, a
\vee
-shaped notch

(resp. a
\wedge
-shaped notch
) in a poset was defined to be a triple
of elements
(a,b,c)
such that
a\lessdot b,a\lessdot c
(resp.
a\gtrdot b,a\gtrdot c
) and
b
and
c
are in different connected components of

P\smallsetminus P_{\leq a}
(resp.
P\smallsetminus P_{\geq a}
). One defines
a signed notch as a pair of notches in this sense.

Definition 15.5
.

Given a signed poset
P
, a
signed notch
in
\widehat{G}(P)
is a pair

(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
such that both
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
are notches
in
\widehat{G}(P)
and, if in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, neither
b
nor
c
is
0
.

Consider again the signed poset
P
from
Figure
28
. Together
(2,3,5)
and
(-2,-3,-5)
form a signed notch.

If
(a,b,c)
is a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
, it follows from the properties of the involution that
(-a,-b,-c)
is also a notch in the type
A
sense, but facing the other way. Consequently, one may assume that
(a,b,c)
is a
\vee
-shaped notch and
(-a,-b,-c)
is a
\wedge
-shaped notch. One may also always assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathrm{sgn}(b)=\mathrm{sgn}(c)
, by relabeling the elements of
\widehat{G}(P)
as necessary.

One next wants to show that closing a signed notch in either
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
or

\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
gives the
\widehat{G}_{B}
or
\widehat{G}_{C}
associated to some other signed
poset. If
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
, the poset
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\}
is the poset obtained by
closing the notch
. It will
often be useful to think of this operation as closing the type
A
notch

(a,b,c)
and then the notch
(-a,-b,-c)
(or vice versa). To legitimize this
point of view, one needs to show the following:

•

(-a,-b,-c)
remains a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}

(Proposition
15.6
)

•

There is a signed poset
P^{\prime}
such that
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})=\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\}
. Such a
P^{\prime}
will be said to have been obtained
from
P
by
closing the signed notch
. (Propositions
15.7

and
15.8
)

The result of closing the signed notch formed by
(2,3,5)
and
(-2,-3,-5)
in

P
shown
in Figure
29
.

2

-3\equiv-5

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{3\equiv 5,-3\equiv-5\}

Figure 29
:
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
from Figure

after closing the signed notch
(2,3,5)
and
(-2,-3,-5)
.

Proposition 15.6
.

If
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
,
(-a,-b,-c)
remains a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
.

Proof.

One knows from
[
9
, Definition 8.5]
that
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
is a poset and thus so is
(\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\})/\{-b\equiv-c\}
, as long as
(-a,-b,-c)
remains a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
. The only way in which
(-a,-b,-c)
can fail to be a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
is if
-b
and
-c
lie in the same connected component of
(\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\})\smallsetminus(\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\})_{\geq-a}
. In that case, there is a path from
-b
to
-c
avoiding
(\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\})_{\geq-a}
. However, since
(-a,-b,-c)
was a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
, this path cannot lift to a path in
\widehat{G}(P)
, so it must pass through the vertex
\{b\equiv c\}
. Therefore at least one of
b
and
c
lies in
\widehat{G}(P)_{\geq-a}
. Without loss of generality, assume
b\in\widehat{G}(P)_{\geq-a}
. But then
b\equiv c\geq-a
in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
, a contradiction. Thus, one must have that
(-a,-b,-c)
is a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
.

∎

With Proposition
15.6
in hand, it is easier to address

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
separately, as the next two propositions.

Proposition 15.7
.

If
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, there is
a signed poset
P^{\prime}\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
such that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})=\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\}
.

Proof.

From Proposition
15.6
, one knows that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{b\equiv c\}
has a notch
(-a,-b,-c)
.
Closing the second notch
(-a,-b,-c)
one obtains

\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})=(\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{b\equiv c\})/\{-b\equiv-c\}\\
=(\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{-b\equiv-c\})/\{b\equiv c\}=\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\},

as the resulting poset is independent of the order in which the notches are closed. Since
\mathrm{sgn}(b)=\mathrm{sgn}(c)
, this poset has an involution
\pm i\mapsto\mp i
such that
i\to j
is sent to
-j\to-i
, since
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
had an involution with this property.

To show that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})=\widehat{G}_{B}(P)/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\}
is really
associated to a signed poset
P^{\prime}
, one must show that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
has the
property that if
i<-i
, then
i<0<-i
. Suppose
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
. It
suffices to consider only the case where
i\not<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. Then,
without loss of generality, one may assume that
b<-i
and
i<c
. First,
consider the case where
a\neq 0
. One then has the situation depicted in
Figure
30
,
where the solid lines are edges in the Hasse diagram of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and the
dashed lines are chains.

i
c
a
b
-b
-a
-c
-i

Figure 30
:
After closing two notches in the proof of
Proposition
15.7

This gives a path from
b
to
c
, via
-i,-c,-a,-b
and
i
. Since

(a,b,c)
is a notch, this path must intersect
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)_{\leq a}
at some
d
.
There are a few cases.

(a)

Suppose
-c\leq d\leq-i
.
Then
-c<c
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. Therefore,
-c<0<c
. Then in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
, one has
b\equiv c<-i
and
i<-b\equiv-c
, so since
-c<0<c
one has
i<-b\equiv-c<0<b\equiv c<-i
, as desired.

(b)

Suppose
i\leq d\leq-b
.
Then in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
one has
i\leq d<a<-i
, so
i<0<-i
and this relation is preserved in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.

(c)

Suppose
i\leq d<c
.
Then one has
i\leq d<c<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, so
i<0<-i
and this relation is preserved in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.

(d)

Suppose
-a=d<a
.
Then
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, so
i<0<-i
and this relation is preserved in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.

In the other case, suppose
a=0
. Then
-b<0<b
. Since
-i>b
,
i<-b
, so
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, so
i<0<-i
and this relation is preserved in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.
∎

Proposition 15.8
.

If
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, there is a signed poset
P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
such that
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})=\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\}
.

Proof.

From Proposition
15.6
, one knows that
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})/\{b\equiv c\}
has a notch
(-a,-b,-c)
.
Closing the second notch
(-a,-b,-c)
one obtains

\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})=(\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})/\{b\equiv c\})/\{-b\equiv-c\}\\
=(\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})/\{-b\equiv-c\})/\{b\equiv c\}=\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})/\{b\equiv c,-b\equiv-c\},

as the resulting poset is independent of the order in which the notches are closed. Since
\mathrm{sgn}(b)=\mathrm{sgn}(c)
, this poset has an involution
\pm i\mapsto\mp i
such that
i\to j
is sent to
-j\to-i
, since
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
had an involution with this property.

It remains to show that
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
has the property that if
i<-i
and
j<-j
(possibly after relabelling) that
i<-j
and
j<-i
. Closing a notch introduces relations and does not remove any relations. There are then two cases:

1.

Suppose
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, but not in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})

and
j<-j
in both
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
and
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.
Then,
without loss of generality, suppose
i<b
and
c<-i
in

\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. Then
-b<-i
and
i<-c
. Then one has the
situation in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
depicted in Figure
31
, where the dashed edges are chains and the solid edges are edges in the Hasse diagram of
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.

2.

.

, as required.

(2.b)

Suppose

\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})

∎

Now that one understands what it means to close a notch in a signed poset, the
natural question is what effect this maneuver has on the semigroup ring.

Proposition 15.9
.

Let
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
be a signed poset and
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. Let
P^{\prime}
be the signed poset obtained from
P
by closing this notch. Then

R_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong\begin{cases}R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{a}^{\delta}t_{b}^{\epsilon}-t_{a}^{\delta}t_{c}^{\epsilon})&\text{if }a\neq 0\\
R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{b}^{\epsilon}-t_{c}^{\epsilon})&\text{if }a=0\\
\end{cases}

where
\delta=\mathrm{sgn}(a)
and
\epsilon=-\mathrm{sgn}(b)
.

Let
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
be a signed poset and
(a,b,c)
and
(-a,-b,-c)
form a signed notch in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. Let
P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
be the signed poset obtained from
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
by closing this notch. Then

R_{P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cong R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{a}^{\delta}t_{b}^{\epsilon}-t_{a}^{\delta}t_{c}^{\epsilon}),

where
\delta=\mathrm{sgn}(a)
and
\epsilon=-\mathrm{sgn}(b)
.

To see how this works, consider once again
P=\{+e_{1}-e_{2},+e_{1}+e_{2},+e_{1}-e_{3},+e_{1}+e_{3},+e_{1}-e_{4},+e_{1}-e_{5},+e_{1}-e_{6},+e_{2}-e_{3},+e_{2}-e_{4},+e_{2}-e_{5},+e_{2}-e_{6},+e_{4}-e_{6},+e_{5}-e_{6},+e_{1}\}
shown in
Figure
28
(page
28
). Closing the notch
(2,3,5)
and
(-2,-3,-5)

gives the poset shown in Figure
29

(page
29
),
Q=\{+e_{1}-e_{2},+e_{1}+e_{2},+e_{1}+e_{3},+e_{1}-e_{3},+e_{1}-e_{4},+e_{1}-e_{6},+e_{2}-e_{3},+e_{2}-e_{4},+e_{3}-e_{6},+e_{4}-e_{6}\}

(with
3\equiv 5
and
3\equiv-5
being renamed
3
and
-3
, respectively).
In type
B
, one has
S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}=k[U_{12},U_{23},U_{\bar{1}3},U_{24},U_{25},U_{46},U_{56},U_{10}]
. Define a map

\psi\colon S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}\to R_{Q}^{\mathrm{rt}}
by

\begin{array}[]{llll}U_{12}\mapsto t_{1}t_{2}^{-1}&U_{23}\mapsto t_{2}t_{3}^{-1}&U_{\bar{1}3}\mapsto t_{1}t_{3}&U_{24}\mapsto t_{2}t_{4}^{-1}\\
U_{25}\mapsto t_{2}t_{3}^{-1}&U_{46}\mapsto t_{4}t_{6}^{-1}&U_{56}\mapsto t_{3}t_{6}^{-1}&U_{10}\mapsto t_{1}\end{array}

Then

\displaystyle R_{Q}^{\mathrm{rt}}

\displaystyle\cong S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}/\ker\psi

\displaystyle\cong S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}/(U_{24}U_{46}-U_{25}U_{56},U_{12}U_{\bar{1}3}U_{25}-U_{10}^{2},U_{23}-U_{25})

\displaystyle=S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}/(I^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}+(U_{23}-U_{25}))

\displaystyle\cong(S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}/I^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P})/(\overline{U}_{23}-\overline{U}_{25})

\displaystyle\cong R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{2}t_{3}^{-1}-t_{2}t_{5}^{-1})

Proof of Proposition
15.9
.

Recall the definitions of
S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}
,
S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
and of the toric ideals
I^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}
and

I^{\mathrm{rt}}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
from Section
14.1
. Let
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
be the signed posets obtained by closing notches in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, respectively. One defines maps from
S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}
and
S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
to the semigroup rings
R_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}=k[K_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{B}]
and
R_{P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}=k[K_{P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}\cap L^{\mathrm{rt}}_{C}]
.

Define a map
\psi\colon S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{P}\to R_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
by

U_{\delta i,\epsilon j}\mapsto\begin{cases}t_{i}^{\delta}t_{j}^{-\epsilon}&i,j\neq c,0\\
t_{i}^{\delta}t_{b}^{-\epsilon}&j=c,i\neq 0\\
t_{b}^{\delta}t_{j}^{-\epsilon}&i=c\\
t_{j}^{-\epsilon}&i=0,j\neq c\\
t_{b}^{-\epsilon}&i=0,j=c\end{cases}

and define a map
\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}\colon S^{\mathrm{rt}}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}\to R_{P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
by

U_{\delta i,\epsilon j}\mapsto\begin{cases}t_{i}^{\delta}t_{j}^{-\epsilon}&i,j\neq c\\
t_{b}^{\delta}t_{j}^{-\epsilon}&i=c\\
t_{i}^{\delta}t_{b}^{-\epsilon}&j=c\end{cases}

One shows that
\ker\psi=I_{P}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
and
\ker\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}=I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
. The argument is the same
for
P
and
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
. To simplify notation, in the remainder of the
proof, write
J
for the kernel,
I
for the toric ideal, let the
variables of the polynomial ring
S
be
U_{ij}
and use

\widehat{G}(P)
to denote
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
or
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
, as applicable.
Thus, one needs to show that
J=I+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
.

Recall that
I
is generated by the cycle binomials of cycles in
\widehat{G}(P)

not fixed by the involution. Let
U(C)
be such a cycle binomial. The
definition of
\varphi
(resp.
\psi
) ensures that
U(C)\in J
, so
I\subset J
.
Both
U_{ab}
and
U_{ac}
have the same image in
R_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
, so
(U_{ab}-U_{ac})\subset J
. Thus
I+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})\subset J
.

Let
P^{+}
be the directed graph that has the same vertices and edges as

\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
, but with the edges
-b\equiv-c\to-a
and
a\to b\equiv c

doubled. The argument in the proof of Theorem
14.5
shows
that
J
is generated by the cycle binomials corresponding to cycles of

P^{+}
not fixed by the involution. (See Figure
33
for
P^{+}

for the
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
from Figure
29
.)

2
3\equiv 5
-3\equiv-5
-2
-1

-4
-6
0

(a)

P^{+}_{C}

-3\equiv-5

(b)

P^{+}_{C}

Figure 33
:
P^{+}_{B}
and

for
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
from
Figure

One then needs to show that for every cycle of
P^{+}
not fixed by the involution, its circuit binomial lies in
I+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
. There are a number of cases.

1.

Suppose
C
is a cycle in
P^{+}
not fixed by the
involution passing through neither
b\equiv c
nor
-b\equiv-c
.
Then
C
lifts to a cycle in
\widehat{G}(P)
, meaning
U(C)\in I_{P}
.

2.

Suppose
C
is a cycle in
P^{+}
not fixed by the
involution, but passing through at least one of
b\equiv c

and
-b\equiv-c
.
One can partition the edges incident to
b\equiv c
and
-b\equiv c
into
E_{b}\sqcup E_{c}
according to whether they lift to edges incident to either
b
or
-b
or to either
c
or
-c
in
\widehat{G}(P)
.

(2.a)

Suppose the edges of
C
incident to
b\equiv c
or

-b\equiv-c
all lie in one of
E_{b}
and
E_{c}
.
Then
C
lifts to a cycle in
\widehat{G}(P)
, so
U(C)\in I
.

(2.b)

Suppose
C
contains an edge from both
E_{b}
and

E_{c}
.
Assume
C
has an edge from each of
E_{b}
and
E_{c}
incident to
b\equiv c
. The other case is symmetric.

Since
(a,b,c)
was a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
, one has that
b
and
c
lie in different connected components of
\widehat{G}(P)\smallsetminus\widehat{G}(P)_{\leq a}
, so
C
must pass through at least one vertex
d\leq a
. Let
\pi_{da}
be a saturated chain between
d
and
a
. Let
C_{b}
be the cycle in
P^{+}
that follows
C
from
b\equiv c
to d, then
\pi_{da}
from
d
to
a
and finishes along the edge in
E_{b}
between
a
and
b\equiv c
. Let
C_{c}
be the cycle in
P^{+}
that follows
C
from
b\equiv c
to
d
, then
\pi_{da}
from
d
to
a
and finishes along the edge in
E_{c}
from
a
to
b\equiv c
.

(2.b.i)

Suppose
C_{b}
and
C_{c}
both lift to cycles in

\widehat{G}(P)
and, with
C_{b}
and
C_{c}
are oriented so
they traverse
\pi_{da}
in opposite directions.
One has

U(C)=U(C_{b})\left(\prod_{e\in W(\pi_{dc})}U_{e}\right)+U(C_{c})\left(\prod_{e\in A(\pi_{bd})}U_{e}\right)\\
+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})\left(\prod_{e\in W(\pi_{dc})}U_{e}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in A(\pi_{bd})}U_{e}\right)\left(\prod_{e\in W(\pi_{da})}U_{e}\right),

where
\pi_{dc}
is the portion of
C_{c}
between
d
and
c
and
\pi_{bd}
is the portion of
C_{b}
between
d
and
b
. Note that it is possible one of
C_{b}
and
C_{c}
is fixed by the involution, in which case its cycle binomial is zero since
W(C)=A(C)
.
Thus,
U(C)\in I+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
, as desired.

(2.b.ii)

Now suppose at least one of
C_{b}
and
C_{c}
does not lift to a cycle
in
\widehat{G}(P)
.
One can use the previous argument to show both
U(C_{b})
and
U(C_{c})
lie in
I_{P}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
. Without loss of generality, suppose
C_{b}
does not lift to a cycle in
\widehat{G}(P)
. (If neither lifts, one repeats the following argument with each cycle.) Since
C_{b}
does not lift to a cycle in
\widehat{G}(P)
, it must pass through
-b\equiv-c
, along one edge in
E_{b}
and one in
E_{c}
. Since
(-a,-b,-c)
was a notch in
\widehat{G}(P)
, there is
d^{\prime}\geq-a\in P^{+}
which
C_{b}
passes through.
Then choose a saturated chain between
d^{\prime}
and
-a
, call it

\pi_{da}^{\prime}
, with the proviso that if
\pi_{da}^{\prime}
includes
a
and
b\equiv c
, it includes the edge in
C
. One can then construct
C_{bb}

and
C_{bc}
as in the previous case and the same argument shows that

U(C_{b})\in I_{P}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
. (However, if
\pi_{da}^{\prime}
coincides
with
C
, it will be the case that one of
C_{bb}
and
C_{cc}
is not
genuinely a cycle, rather one will have a chain from (say)
b
to
d^{\prime}
and then return to
b
down the same chain, in which case
U(C_{bb})=0
.)

Thus,
J=I+(U_{ab}-U_{ac})
.

To finish the proof, one sees that

\displaystyle R_{P^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rt}}

\displaystyle\cong S_{P}/\ker\psi

\displaystyle\cong S_{P}/(I_{P}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac}))

\displaystyle\cong(S_{P}/I_{P})/(\bar{U}_{ab}-\bar{U}_{ac})

\displaystyle\cong R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{a}^{\mathrm{sgn}(a)}t_{b}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b)}-t_{a}^{\mathrm{sgn}(a)}t_{c}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(c)}),

where
t_{a}=1
if
a=0
,
and

\displaystyle R_{P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}

\displaystyle\cong S_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}/\ker\psi^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}

\displaystyle\cong S_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}/(I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}+(U_{ab}-U_{ac}))

\displaystyle\cong(S_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}/I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}})/(\bar{U}_{ab}-\bar{U}_{ac})

\displaystyle\cong R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}/(t_{a}^{\mathrm{sgn}(a)}t_{b}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b)}-t_{a}^{\mathrm{sgn}(a)}t_{c}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(c)}).

∎

15.3
Strongly Planar Signed Posets

Having looked at the notion of opening notches and its impact on
R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
, one
is ready to show that a certain class of signed posets have
R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
a complete
intersection, implying that the numerator of
\Psi_{P}
factors.

Definition 15.10
.

A poset is said to be
strongly planar
if, after the addition of a maximal element

\hat{1}
and a minimal element
\hat{0}
, there is an embedding of its
Hasse diagram in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that is planar and has the property that if
a<_{P}b
,
the
y
-coordinate of
a
is smaller than that of
b
. A signed poset

P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
P\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) will be said to be
strongly
planar
if

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is strongly planar.

As an example, consider
P=\{+e_{1}+e_{2},+e_{1},+e_{2}\}
. Figure
34
shows
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)

and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. One sees that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
is strongly planar, but
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is not.

Definition 15.11
.

An embedding of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is said to be
centrally
symmetric
if it is fixed by the map
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,-y)
.

The main result of this section will be the following.

Theorem 15.12
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset such
that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has an embedding in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
which is
strongly planar. Then

•

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has an embedding
that is both centrally symmetric and strongly planar,

•

R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}}
(resp.
R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
) is a complete intersection, with
I_{P}

(resp.
I_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
) generated by the cycle binomials of the cycles in

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) defining the faces of the graph, and

•

one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{P}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod_{\rho}(1-\bm{x}^{\rho})}{\prod_{e}(1-x_{a}^{\delta}x_{b}^{-\epsilon})}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hilb}(R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\prod_{\rho}(1-\bm{x}^{\rho})}{\prod_{e}(1-x_{a}^{\delta}x_{b}^{-\epsilon})},

where

,
x_{0}
is taken to be
1
and

\bm{x}^{\rho}=x_{\min(\rho)}^{\mathrm{sgn}(\min(\rho))}x_{\max(\rho)}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\max(\rho))}.

The edges which correspond to the notches that are opened form a set of

disconnecting chains
splitting
\widehat{G}(P)
into biconnected components.

Definition 15.13
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset. A

disconnecting chain
of
\widehat{G}(P)
is a chain
c_{1}\lessdot c_{2}\lessdot\dots\lessdot c_{k}
such that removing
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}
and

-c_{k}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot-c_{1}
breaks
\widehat{G}(P)
into three connected components.

The first step in the proof is to locate a region to work with.

Proposition 15.14
.

Suppose
Q
is a strongly planar poset whose Hasse diagram is connected.
Then
Q
has a region
\rho
such that any vertex other than the maximum
and minimum element of
\rho
in the right border of
\rho
is in the
border of no other region. Such a region is called a
rightmost
region
.

By considering a strongly planar poset, one has the luxury of assuming a
given strongly planar embedding, allowing sensible notions of “left” and
“right”.

Definition 15.15
.

Suppose
Q
is a strongly planar poset. The
rightmost cover
of
x\in Q
is the
y
that covers
x
such that if one traversed a small circle around
x
counterclockwise (starting from the bottom, say) one passes the edge leading to
y
last. The
rightmost lower cover
is the
z\lessdot x
such that the edge leading to
z
is encountered first when traveling clockwise from the top of the circle.

Proof of Proposition
15.14
.

Without loss of generality, one may assume that every vertex in
Q
has degree
at least two, since one makes this assumption of
\widehat{G}(P)
.
Since
Q
is strongly planar, the poset
\widehat{Q}
obtained by adding a
\hat{0}
and
\hat{1}
to
Q
is also planar. Construct a saturated chain
\hat{0}\lessdot c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}\lessdot\hat{1}
such that
c_{i+1}
is the rightmost cover of
c_{i}
for all
i
and
c_{1}
is the rightmost cover of
\hat{0}
.

Let
a=c_{i}
, where
i
is maximal such that
c_{i}
is covered by at least
two elements. (Since
c_{1}
is a minimal element of
Q
, and
Q
has been
assumed to have no vertices of degree 1, it has at least two covers, so such
an
a
exists.) Construct a chain
a\lessdot b_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot b_{\ell}\lessdot\hat{1}
where
b_{1}
is the cover of
a
that is rightmost but one and
b_{i+1}
is the rightmost cover of
b_{i}
.

Claim:

c_{k}
is the rightmost lower cover of
\hat{1}
in
\widehat{Q}
.

Suppose not and
d\lessdot\hat{1}
is to the right of
c_{k}
. Then
d
must lie above some minimal element of
Q
, say
f
. Since
Q
is strongly planar, a maximal chain from
f
to
d
must intersect
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}
. Let
j
be maximal such that
c_{j}
is in this chain from
f
to
d
. Since
d\neq c_{k}
, one must have that
j\neq k
. Since
d
is to the right of
c_{k}
, the chain must continue along the rightmost cover of
c_{j}
. However, this is
c_{j+1}
, contradicting the maximality of
j
.

Claim:

b_{\ell}=c_{k}
.

Suppose not. Then
c_{k}
has degree
\geq 2
and it was maximal in
Q
, so it must cover some
d\neq c_{k-1}
. Then
Q
has a minimal element
a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime}<d
. As
Q
is
strongly planar, a saturated chain from
a^{\prime}
to
d
must include either some

b_{i}
or
c_{j}
,
j<k
. If the chain includes
b_{i}
, then there must be some

m
where
b_{m+1}
is not the rightmost cover of
b_{m}
, a contradiction. If

c_{j}
is in the chain, but no
b_{i}
, then, since
d\neq c_{k}
, there must be
an
i
such that
c_{n}>a
and
c_{n}
has more than two covers, contradicting
that
a=c_{i}
was maximal with this property.

Let
\rho
be the region enclosed by the
b_{i}
and
c_{j}
. Note that
b_{\ell}=c_{k}
need not be the maximal element of
\rho
. However, by construction,
a
will be the minimal element of
\rho
.

Claim:
The
c_{j}
with
c_{j}>a
are rightmost in
Q
, i.e. they are not in the left border of any region.

Suppose
c_{j}
is on the right border of
\rho
(and is not
\max(\rho)
or

\min(\rho)
), that
c_{j}
is on the left border of some region
\sigma
and
that

j
is minimal such that this is the case. Then, since
c_{j+1}
is the
rightmost cover of
c_{j}
, the edge
(c_{j},c_{j+1})
must be in the left
border of
\sigma
. Consequently,
c_{j}
cannot be the minimal element of

\sigma
. Then, by the minimality of
j
, there is some
d\lessdot c_{j}
such
that
d
is to the right of
c_{j-1}
, as depicted in
Figure
39

c_{j}
c_{j-1}
d
c_{j+1}
\sigma
\rho

Figure 39
:
The situation in the last claim of the proof of
Proposition
15.14

The region
\sigma
must have a minimum element, call it
f
. Then
f\geq g
for some
g
that is minimal in
Q
. Then, since
Q
is strongly planar and
c_{1}
is the rightmost minimal element of
Q
, a saturated chain from
g
to
f
must pass through some
c_{i}
. But then, since
c_{i+1}
is the rightmost cover of
c_{i}
, one must have that
f
is one of the
c
’s, a contradiction.
∎

One can now use the previous result to prove a few lemmas specific to
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.

Lemma 15.16
.

Suppose
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
is a
\Phi_{C_{n}}
-signed poset such that
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is strongly planar. Then there is at most one
i
such that
\pm 2e_{i}
corresponds to an edge in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.

Proof.

Suppose
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
is a signed poset such that
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is
strongly planar and
i\lessdot-i
and
j\lessdot-j
. Then one must have

i<-j
and
j<-i
. Then if
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is embedded in the plane (so
that if
a<b
, then
y_{a}<y_{b}
), there must be a path from
i
to
-j

and
j
to
-i
, but these paths must intersect. For
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
to be
planar, they must intersect at a vertex, say
k
. But then
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})

would cease to be the Hasse diagram of a poset, as the relation
i<-i
would follow by transitivity from
i<k
and
k<-i
. Thus, such a
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
cannot be strongly planar.
∎

Lemma 15.17
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset such
that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is strongly planar. Then, if

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
) encloses more than one region, a cycle
defining a rightmost region of
\widehat{G}(P)
is not fixed by the involution.

Proof.

Suppose
C
is a cycle in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) that encloses a rightmost region,
\rho
. Suppose
C
is fixed by the involution. Let
\sigma
be a region to the immediate left of
\rho
. The type
B
and type
C
cases are slightly different.

First, suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
. There are two cases.

1.

Suppose
0
is not in the left border of
\rho
.
Let
e
be an edge that lies in the left border of
\rho
and the right border of
\sigma
. Since
0
is not in the left border of
\rho
, an edge and its image under the involution cannot both be in the left border. Therefore, since
\rho
is fixed by the involution,
\iota(e)
must lie in the right border of
\rho
. But
\iota(e)
is also in the border of
\iota(\sigma)
, contradicting that
\rho
is a rightmost region.

2.

Suppose
0
is in the left border of
\rho
.
Then if
j
is in the left border,
-j
must also be in the left border, since
\rho
is fixed by the involution and
\pm j<0<\mp j
. Then the left border is fixed by the involution. Since
\rho
itself is fixed by the involution, this means that the right border must also be fixed and thus symmetric, a contradiction, since
0
is in the left border, not the right.

Next, consider
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
. There are two cases.

1.

Suppose there is no edge of the form
(i,-i)
in the left border
of
\rho
.
Since
\sigma
is to the left of
\rho
, there is some
edge
e
that lies in the left border of
\rho
and the right
border of
\sigma
. Since
\rho
is fixed by the involution,
\iota(e)

lies in the border of
\rho
. Since there is no edge of the form

(i,-i)
in the left border, it is not symmetric and
\iota(e)
cannot
be in the left border, so it is in the right border. But,

\iota(e)
is in the border of
\iota(\sigma)
, meaning
\rho
cannot be a rightmost region, a contradiction.

2.

Suppose there is an edge
(i,-i)
in the left border of

\rho
.

Then, by the definition of
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, if
j
is in the left
border of
\rho
, then
-j
is also in the left border of
\rho
.
From Lemma
15.16
, one knows that the right
border of
\rho
does not include an edge
(k,-k)
, so there must
be some
k
in the right border such that
-k
is not in the right
border. (In fact, the absence of such an edge means only one of
k

and
-k
is in the right border for any
k
that is not maximal or
minimal in
C
.) Then
\iota(k)=-k
is a vertex in
\iota(C)
, which cannot be
C
, so
C
is not fixed by the involution.

∎

Proposition 15.18
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset. Then
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}
) has a strongly planar embedding if and only if it has a centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding.

Proof.

One needs to show only that if
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is
strongly planar, it has a centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding.
Instead, prove a slightly stronger statement, namely that if
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)

(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has a strongly planar embedding, it also has a
centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding such that the outer border of

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is the same in both the first strongly
planar embedding and the centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding.

Induct on the number of regions in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
). There are two base cases.

First, suppose
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) consists of a single region, bounded by a single cycle. One needs to address type
B
and type
C
separately.

•

In type
C
, consider
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. Since it is strongly planar,
it has a single maximum and a single minimum, which must be
i
and
-i
.

Claim:
There is no
j\neq i
such that
j<-j
or
-j<j
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.

Suppose not. Since
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
consists of a single cycle, one has that
either
i<j<-j<-i
, in which case one border of
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is
fixed by the involution, so
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
must have more than one
region, a contradiction, or
j
lies in the left border of
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
and
-j
lies in
the right border of
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. In the latter case, one must have
that the left border of
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is sent to the right border under
the involution, i.e. if
i\lessdot c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}\lessdot-i
is the left border, then
i\lessdot-c_{k}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot-c_{1}\lessdot-i
is the right border. However, it is then
impossible that
j<-j
since
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
encloses a single region.

Thus, when
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is strongly planar and encloses a single
region,
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
can be embedded centrally symmetrically by evenly spacing the vertices around a circle centered at the origin with
-i
at the top and
-i
at the bottom.

•

In type
B
, consider
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. Since
i<-i
, one must have

i<0<-i
, meaning either the left or right border of

\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
is symmetric about
0
, so it is fixed by the
involution. But then
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
must have more than one region,
contradicting the assumption.

Next, consider the second base case where
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) consists of two
regions. From Lemma
15.17
, one knows that the rightmost
region
\rho
is not fixed by the involution. Therefore, the border between the
two regions consists of a chain fixed by the involution and the two regions are
exchanged by the involution. One then has the scenario depicted in
Figure
40
.

\rho
\iota(\rho)
c
d
e
\iota(e)
\iota(d)

Figure 40
:
Two regions exchanged by the involution separated by a chain,
c
, fixed
by the involution

One sees that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) (when translated appropriately) is centrally symmetric and strongly planar.

Now suppose
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) encloses more than two regions. Then, by Proposition
15.14
, it has a rightmost region, call it
\rho
. The right border of
\rho
is defined by some chain
\min(\rho)\lessdot c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}\lessdot\max(\rho)
. Since
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is a poset and
\rho
is enclosed by a cycle,
k\geq 1
. Since
\rho
is rightmost, by definition each edge in its right border lies in no cycle defining a region other than
\rho
. The same then must be true of the image of the right border of
\rho
in
\iota(\rho)
. Deleting
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k},-c_{1},\ldots,-c_{k}
and the incident edges gives
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) for a signed poset
P^{\prime}

Since
\widehat{G}(P)
was strongly planar,
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
is strongly planar. By induction,
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
has a centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding. The left border of
\rho
is now part of the right border of
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
, meaning the chain
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}
and be attached to form a new region on the right of
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
and similarly for
-c_{k}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot-c_{1}
on the left. Since
\pm\min(\rho)
and
\pm\max(\rho)
are positioned so as to be centrally symmetric, it is possible to place the chains in such a way as to preserve central symmetry. Replacing the chains in
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
gives a centrally symmetric strongly planar embedding of
\widehat{G}(P)
.
∎

Lemma 15.19
.

Suppose
P\subset\Phi_{B_{n}}
(resp.
P\subset\Phi_{C_{n}}
) is a signed poset such that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is centrally symmetric and strongly planar. Suppose
\rho
is a rightmost region of
\widehat{G}(P)
and
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k}
is the portion of the left border of
\rho
which is part of the right border of some other region(s), with
k\geq 2
and
c_{k}\neq-c_{k-1}
(or
c_{1}\neq-c_{2}
). Then
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) is obtained from
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
) for some signed poset
P^{\prime}
by closing a signed notch
(c_{k-1},c_{k},c_{k}^{\prime})
and
(-c_{k-1},-c_{k},-c_{k}^{\prime})
or a signed notch
(c_{2},c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime})
and
(-c_{2},-c_{1},-c_{1}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Begin by observing that in the type
B
case, one of
c_{1}
and
c_{k}

is nonzero, and in the type
C
case, at least one of
c_{k}\neq-c_{k-1}
and
c_{1}\neq-c_{2}
holds. Without loss of generality, assume
c_{k}\neq 0
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
c_{k}\neq-c_{k-1}
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. (The argument in the other cases is symmetric.)

Construct
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
) by replacing
c_{k}
by
c_{k}
and
c_{k}^{\prime}
, with an edge between
c_{k-1}
and each of
c_{k}
and
c_{k}^{\prime}
, with the edges incident to
c_{k}
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) that are not in the border of
\rho
moved to be incident to
c_{k}^{\prime}
. Replace
-c_{k}
by
-c_{k}
and
-c_{k}^{\prime}
and partition the edges incident to
-c_{k}
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) in the same way. By construction,
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime}c)
) will be centrally symmetric and strongly planar and have an involution sending
i<j
to
-j<-i
(and fixing
0
in the type
B
case). Then to show
P^{\prime}
is really a signed poset, one needs to check the conditions in Proposition
8.5
and Proposition
8.6
.

First, consider the type
B
case. Suppose
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
. Since opening a notch does not introduce relations, one must have that
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, so
i<0<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
. Suppose one does not have
-i>0
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
. There are two cases where
0<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
could fail to lift to
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
-i>c_{k}
and
0<c_{k}^{\prime}
, but
0\not<-i
.

By construction (keeping in mind that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
is centrally symmetric and strongly planar),
0
must be in the right border of
\rho
and
c_{k}^{\prime}=\max(\rho)
. However
0
being in the right border contradicts central symmetry.

•

Suppose
-i>-c_{k}
and
0<-c_{k}^{\prime}
, but
0\not<-i
.

By construction, one has that
0
is in the part of the border of

\iota(\rho)
in both
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
not shared by any other region. Thus, since
i<0
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, one must have
i<0
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
, contradicting that
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
.

In the type
C
case, one must check that if
i<-i
and
j<-j
in

\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
, then
i<-j
and
j<-i
.

First, observe that if
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
, then
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
. (If
i=c_{k}^{\prime}
in
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
, then
c_{k}<-c_{k}
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
.) Now suppose that
i<-i
and
j<-j
in
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
. Then
i<-j
and
j<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
(possibly vacuously if
i=j
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
). Should these relations fail to lift to
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
, one is in one of the following two cases.

1.

Suppose
i\leq c_{k}
and
-j\geq c_{k}^{\prime}
.

There are several subcases.

(1.a)

Suppose
i<c_{k-1}
.
Then
i<-j
and one is done.

(1.b)

Suppose
i<c_{k}
, but
i\not<c_{k-1}
.
In
other words,
i
is on the right border of
\rho
and
c_{k}=\max(\rho)
. Since
\rho
is not fixed by the involution
and is a right region,
-i
is not on the right border of

\rho
. (If it were, either
\rho
would contain an edge
(k,-k)
and thus not be a right region, or
i=\min(\rho)
,
-i=\max(\rho)
and
\rho
would be cut out by a cycle fixed by the involution, since
\max(\rho)
has degree 2, by construction.) Therefore, one cannot have that
i<-i
in
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, a contradiction.

(1.c)

Suppose
i=c_{k}
.
Then
c_{k}<-c_{k}
, but, by
construction of
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, elements in the
border of
\rho
other than
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k-1}
are not in the border of any other region in

\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
and
-c_{k}
is in the border of
\iota(\rho)
, a
contradiction, a contradiction.

2.

Suppose
i\leq c_{k}^{\prime}
and
-j\geq c_{k}
.
In this case,
-j
must be
on the outer border of
\rho
, as it is not one of
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k-1}
. Then, if
j<-j
, one has that either
-c_{k}<c_{k}
or
-c_{1}<c_{1}
. In the latter case, it follows that
-c_{k}<c_{k}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, suppose
-j=c_{k}
. Consider two cases.

(2.a)

There is a chain from
-c_{k}
to
c_{k}
which is
not fixed by the involution.
This chain and its image form a cycle fixed by the involution, having
c_{k}
as its maximum and
-c_{k}
as its minimum. But then
c_{k}
, which has degree two, must cover two elements in this cycle, meaning
c_{k}=\max(\rho)
and this cycle is the one that cuts out
\rho
. This is a contradiction, since
\rho
is not fixed by the involution.

(2.b)

There is a unique chain from
-c_{k}
to
c_{k}
.
By construction of
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime})
, this chain passes through
c_{k-1}
and
-c_{k-1}
and extends uniquely to a maximal chain from
\min(\iota(\rho))
to
\max(\rho)
. On the other hand, since
i<-i
, there is a chain from
-i
to
i
. Since
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
is centrally symmetric and strongly planar, this chain must pass through the chain from
\min(i\rho))
to
\max(\rho)
, meaning
-c_{k-1}<-i
and
i<c_{k-1}
, so
-c_{k}<-i
and
i<c_{k}
, as desired.

Lastly, one must show that
(c_{k-1},c_{k},c_{k}^{\prime})
and
(-c_{k-1},-c_{k},-c_{k}^{\prime})
forms a signed notch in
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
. However, this follows from the fact
c_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot c_{k-1}
and
-c_{k-1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot-c_{1}
are disconnecting chains in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P^{\prime}))
.

∎

One is finally ready to prove Theorem
15.12
.

Proof of Theorem
15.12
.

Begin by realizing one may assume courtesy of Proposition
15.18
that
\widehat{G}(P)
is embedded in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
so as to
be centrally symmetric and strongly planar. Next, recall that a centrally
symmetric strongly planar
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
contains at least two regions. If a
centrally symmetric strongly planar
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
has no region not fixed by
the involution, central symmetry guarantees it consists of one region, cut
out by a cycle fixed by the involution. This fixed cycle is the only cycle of

\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
, so the toric ideal
I^{\mathrm{rt}}_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}
is the zero ideal and
R_{{P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}}}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is a complete intersection.

Thus, one may assume
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has at least one region
not fixed by the involution. Induct on the number of two-element orbits of
regions under the involution of
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
). Since
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}(P)
) is centrally symmetric and strongly planar, it has a rightmost region
\rho
and
\rho
is not fixed by the involution.

As a base case, consider the case where
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
(resp.
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
) has only one orbit. The case where
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
consists of a single orbit of one region has already been addressed. Thus, one may assume in both the
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
and
\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
cases that there are precisely two regions, which are exchanged by the involution. Call them
\rho
and
\iota(\rho)
, with
\rho
being the right region. There are two cases.

1.

The regions
\rho
and
\iota(\rho)
share an edge and so
intersect along a chain.
(See Figure
41(a)
.)

\iota(\rho)

Then

, its image under the involution, which defines
\iota(\rho)
,

(resp.

) is generated by the cycle defining

(resp.

) is a complete intersection.

The regions
\rho
and
\iota(\rho)
do not share an edge and
instead share a single vertex.
(See Figure

. Note that this case only arises in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)

).

, are the only cycles in
\widehat{G}_{B}(P)
, so

is a principal ideal and

is a complete intersection.

Suppose
\widehat{G}(P)
has
n>1
orbits of regions and the result holds for posets
P^{\prime}
such that
\widehat{G}(P^{\prime})
has

orbits of regions not fixed by the involution. Let

\widehat{G}(P)

. Note that the removal of

\widehat{G}(P)
.

c_{j_{1}}

. See

.) Induct on

.

.

Postpone discussing the base case and suppose
\ell\geq 3
.

Claim:

P
is obtained by closing a notch in some signed poset

such that
\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})
(resp.

) has a right region

such that

There are three cases.

Suppose
c_{j_{\ell}-1}\neq-c_{j_{\ell}}

.

By Lemma
15.19
, there is a signed poset
P^{\prime}
such that

(c_{j_{\ell}-1},c_{j_{\ell}}^{\prime},c_{j_{\ell}})

\widehat{G}_{B}(P^{\prime})

The result then follows by induction.

c_{\ell-1}=-c_{\ell}
. This case can only

. Then since
\ell\geq 3
, from

, one knows that
c_{1}\neq-c_{2}
.

Once again, from Lemma

, there is a

P^{\prime}

\widehat{G}_{C}({P^{\scriptscriptstyle\vee}})
